The candidate runs from a copy on the sandbox's `/tmp` with `node_modules` linked to it, because a build writes into its own folder. A failed install fails the check and names why. A build that fetches from the network (`next/font/google` downloading a font, say) fails in the sandbox by design: the repository vendors what it needs, or its goal tests don't run that build. A goal test may drive a page in a headless browser: the browsers sit at `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH`, which the test's otherwise empty environment keeps, and the candidate's `@playwright/test` must match the image's version (amended 2026-10-25, Dorian's decision on goal wfa-sources-v1). Database tests are out of scope. A test covers a Node candidate built offline from its locked dependencies, a candidate with no lockfile installing nothing, and the install container getting only the three files (amended 2026-10-05).
8. The holdout needs no secret (amended 2026-10-05, Dorian's choice: a manual step per goal, or one key per repository or organization, defeats the point). `goal_new.py holdout` writes it to `~/.goal-holdouts/<id>.py` on the machine the run uses, outside every repository, and only its SHA-256 goes into the goal folder Dorian approves. It stays there through the build. When the goal tests pass, `goal_run.py finalize` reads it with code, refuses it unless it matches the approved hash, and commits it as `goal-runs/<id>/holdout.py` beside the run record, so it reaches the repository only in the goal pull request. `goal-holdout` reads it from that pull request's merge candidate, checks the hash again, and deletes the file from the candidate before the candidate runs, so the code under test can't open it. The secret `GOAL_<ID>_HOLDOUT` still works where one is set. The accepted trade: the holdout sits on a machine the building agent can reach for the whole run, so only its instructions keep it from reading it, and the hash check makes a swapped holdout fail loudly. A test covers the finalize step carrying a matching holdout and refusing a missing or swapped one, the envelope allowing the finalize commit with its holdout and nothing else, and `goal-holdout` running the carried holdout with the file gone before the candidate runs.
9. A pnpm app in a subfolder runs too (amended 2026-10-24, from goal `wfa-web-v1` in workforce-automation, whose tests run `pnpm --dir web install --frozen-lockfile --offline` and the build). Every folder one or two levels below the root with its own `pnpm-lock.yaml` is a pnpm project. Each must pin pnpm exactly in its `package.json` `packageManager`, and all must pin the same version, or the check refuses and names the folder. Its packages install in the same networked install container, from that folder's `package.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `.npmrc` and `pnpm-workspace.yaml` only, through corepack with `--frozen-lockfile --ignore-scripts`, hoisted. In the sandbox the folder's `node_modules` is copied into the candidate's copy on `/tmp`, not linked, because pnpm recreates a `node_modules` it reaches through a symlink and `/deps` is read-only, and a `pnpm` on `PATH` runs the pinned version from the copied corepack cache with the network off. So the goal test's own `pnpm install --offline` finds everything current and changes nothing. A root-level pnpm workspace is still `goal_local`'s (GO-010.2). Tests cover a pnpm app in `web/` with no root `package.json` installing and building offline in the sandbox, the pin rules, the install container getting that folder's manifest files only, and the copied `node_modules` and the pinned `pnpm` in the sandbox.

### GO-003 — `/gstack-goal` runs the plan onto a goal branch, and `main` moves once

**Status:** done. Criteria 1, 3, 4 and 8 in PR #104 (`e297a22`), criterion 5 and the sealed goal tests in PR #105 (`e9602e4`), criterion 6 and the runner's half of criterion 7 in PR #106 (`761f56a`), each merged by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` after Dorian's approving review at the head, recorded by `record-release` as `agent_merge_autonomous`; criteria 2 and 7's settings made by Dorian on 2 October 2026. Removed history (review narrative and evidence) is kept in [`DESIGN.md` at `ef74ce8`](https://github.com/MoxyWolfLLC/moxywolf-plugins/blob/ef74ce8/DESIGN.md).

**Settings (Dorian, 2 October 2026):** the `goal-holdout` environment limited to `main`; the `goal-branches` ruleset on `goal/*` (no deletion or force push, pull requests only, `goal-envelope` from GitHub Actions, an empty bypass list, creation allowed); `main-gate` requiring `tests`, `goal-envelope`, `goal-tests` and `goal-holdout` from GitHub Actions, branches up to date, stale approvals dismissed and code-owner review. Criterion 9's tests that need those rulesets to refuse something run in B-e.

**Links introduced:** the goal-branch-to-main link. A goal's changes reach `main` together, reviewed as a whole, after its goal tests and holdout passed at one head, because the goal branch reaches `main` only through a pull request with its own cross-vendor review and required checks.

1. `/gstack-goal <id>` runs `goal_brief.py verify <id>`, records the result, and opens `goal/<id>` from `main`.
2. A ruleset on `goal/*` requires a pull request and the envelope check (GO-004.1), and blocks direct and force pushes. The bot can't bypass it.
3. The runner refuses to start when the builder's model family drafted the goal tests or read them (GO-002.1 and .4), or when `agent_token.py permissions` shows a secrets permission (GO-002.3). Items run in `PLAN.md`'s order, one at a time, each through the existing `/gstack-build` loop with its pull request targeting `goal/<id>`. Every item is built, even after the goal tests pass, up to Max items; the finish waits for the last one, because an item the tests don't reach is still part of the approved goal. The cross-vendor review rules stay as they are. The cap is the brief's Max review rounds, never more than three, so DR-113's escalation after three blocking rounds still holds.
4. After each item merges to `goal/<id>`, the runner runs the goal tests at the new head and records which pass. An `invariant` test that fails, or an `outcome` test that passed and now fails, stops the run.
5. Every goal check (`goal-envelope`, `goal-tests`, `goal-holdout`) is a workflow under `.github/workflows/` on `pull_request_target`, so its definition comes from the base branch, and it checks out the enforcement scripts and `goals/<id>/` from `main` by SHA, never from the pull request's head. `goal-holdout` runs only on pull requests into `main`, in the `goal-holdout` environment. Each job resolves the pull request's head SHA and GitHub's merge candidate at start, evaluates that candidate, re-reads the pull request before publishing, and publishes nothing if the head or base moved (the move starts a fresh run). It publishes its verdict as a check run on the head SHA through the workflow's own `GITHUB_TOKEN`, so the check comes from GitHub Actions. Each ruleset that requires one names GitHub Actions as its only accepted source, so a status the bot posts under the same name doesn't satisfy it, and a verdict on one head never satisfies another. Only `goal-tests` and `goal-holdout` execute candidate code, and only under GO-002.3's isolation. Every token the runner and builders use in a goal run is minted by `agent_token.py` with `contents` and `pull_requests` only and no `workflows` permission, so GitHub refuses any push that adds or changes a workflow file, and no agent workflow runs before a merge-time check sees it.
6. When every goal test passes, the runner opens the pull request from `goal/<id>` to `main`. The `goal-holdout` workflow (under `.github/`, so a `CODEOWNERS` path) runs on that pull request's merge candidate, checks the holdout against `holdout.sha256` from `main` (the carried holdout of GO-002.8, or the secret where one is set), runs it, and reports the check `goal-holdout`. The ruleset requires that check from the GitHub Actions source only. A holdout failure stops the run and is reported as a possible reward hack. The check names the failing holdout tests and their error lines, never the holdout's source, so the failure can be diagnosed; that holdout is then spent (amended 2026-10-05).
7. The pull request into `main` gets a fresh cross-vendor review of the whole diff at its head with coverage `checked`, `tests` green, the envelope check over the whole diff against `main`'s copy of the brief, a required check that runs the goal tests from `main`'s copy of `goals/<id>/`, a passing `goal-holdout` check, and, while the staged approvals in this objective's introduction apply, Dorian's approving review at the head, which GitHub enforces as code-owner review because the pull request carries the goal's run record, `goal-runs/<id>/RESULT.md`, and `goal-runs/` is a `CODEOWNERS` path. `goal-envelope` fails a goal pull request into `main` that doesn't carry it, so the review can't be skipped by leaving the record out. It's outside `goals/<id>/`, so writing it doesn't change the approved tree. Once the holdout's preconditions are met, the runner opens one finalize pull request into `goal/<id>` whose only change is that file; the envelope check allows `goal-runs/<id>/RESULT.md` in a pull request that changes nothing else, and no other `CODEOWNERS` path anywhere. The ruleset dismisses stale approvals on push, so his approval holds only at the head he approved, and a withdrawn approval blocks the merge. Every required check runs on GitHub's merge candidate, and the ruleset requires the branch up to date with `main` before merging, so a move of `main` reruns them. It merges and is recorded through `record-release` like any other change.
8. A run ends in one of three outcomes, written to the run record and the final digest: `complete` (merged to `main`), `stopped` (with the stop condition that fired), or `exhausted` (the plan or Max items ran out with goal tests still failing). Only `complete` is success.
9. Tests with stub builds cover: a plan run in order; a regression that stops the run; a holdout failure; an exhausted plan; a direct push to `goal/<id>` (refused by the ruleset, quoted in B-e); an item pull request that replaces `goal_envelope.py` or its workflow with an unconditional pass, which still fails the check because `main`'s copy runs; a goal tree that changed mid-run (stopped); a builder from the drafting or the reading family (refused); a token with a secrets permission (refused); a holdout whose hash differs from `holdout.sha256` (the `goal-holdout` job fails); candidate code that tries to read the holdout file, read the environment or open a network connection during the holdout job, each of which fails; a push after the holdout passed, which reruns `goal-holdout`; Dorian's approval given after the checks, withdrawn after them, and followed by a push, each read by GitHub at the head; the whole finish, from the last item through the finalize pull request to his approval and the merge; a verdict published for one head that doesn't satisfy the next; a `goal-holdout` status posted by the bot, which doesn't satisfy the ruleset (quoted in B-e); `main` moving after the checks passed, which blocks the merge until they rerun.
10. A goal run's token reads what a private repository hides (amended 2026-10-05). `agent_token.py --goal-run` mints contents and pull_requests write plus checks, actions, environments and deployments read, because GitHub hides all four from a token without those scopes, and a run reads them to decide a merge (GO-005.4) and to see what a push would set off (GO-005.5). Read scopes only: the token still can't write a workflow or touch a secret, and `goal_run.py start` still refuses one that can. `goal_run.py` takes the governed repository from its checkout's origin, so a run in another repository needs no `--repo`.
11. `goal_run.py merged` runs the goal tests at the merged head in the same container, with the candidate's dependencies installed, that `goal-tests` uses in CI and baseline uses at start (added 2026-10-05). `test_goal_run.py` checks that `merged` judges through `goal_brief.sandboxed`.
12. Delivery is the completion rule for a goal in delivery mode (accepted 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`). Each item keeps a durable record, referencing existing records rather than copying them, of its phase (preflight, build, verify, review, prepare release, merge, record and synchronize, finish), candidate head and base, context digest, source bindings, test receipts, review chain, required checks, PR identity, release handoff, merge record and synchronization result. After a session or tool restart the runner resumes from that record and revalidates head, base, authority, budget, context and prerequisites before advancing. A timed-out push or merge is inspected remotely before any retry; a merge that succeeded but was not recorded is recorded, never merged again. The goal is `complete` only when every planned item and its declared release companion are merged and recorded, goal tests and holdout rules pass, and every named required working copy is synchronized; otherwise the record reports the applicable phase, blocker or `sync_pending` naming the checkout and reason. Routine in-scope failures (a failed test, a demonstrated review blocker, a missing required version bump, outdated evidence after an authorized fix, an ordinary sync from `main`) are the agent's to fix within the original limits; a checkpoint is history, not a prohibition on corrective commits.

### GO-004 — The envelope is enforced outside the agent, and the runner stops spend near the cap

**Status:** done. Criteria 1, 3 and 4 in PR #102 (`d3147ef`) and criterion 2, rewritten on Dorian's decision, in PR #103 (`df0bb1c`), each merged by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` after his approving review at the head, recorded by `record-release` as `agent_merge_autonomous`; reviews `20261001-210512-a89e96e-ngr7o8bw` and `20261001-214159-92f8389-40wbjpfc` (codex), both `fixes_verified` in 2 rounds. The runner's read of the ledger before each step is GO-003's `goal_run.py next`.

1. `goal_envelope.py` is a required check on every pull request into a `goal/` branch and on the goal pull request into `main`. It fails, naming each file, when a changed file is outside the brief's Allowed paths or matches a `CODEOWNERS` path. It reads the brief from `main`, never the branch, and examines only files changed by head commits not on `main`, so commits arriving from `main` aren't the goal's. Keeping `goal/<id>` up to date is a pull request into it from a branch at `main`'s tip plus one empty commit, which carries no goal-authored change and so passes (the empty commit gives the head its own check suite; a head that is `main`'s own commit has its checks filed with `main`'s push, where they don't count). A conflict in that merge stops the run. A sync pull request merges under DR-113's authority, without a new cross-vendor review, once the envelope check finds no goal-authored commits and `goal-tests` is green, because every commit it carries was already reviewed into `main`. `goal_run.py may --action merge --sync 1` allows it only when its head changes no file against `main` and its checks are green for that pull request. A sync pull request with any goal-authored commit is an item pull request and follows GO-005.4. The goal never moves the marketplace's top-level version, because `main` moves that line too and every sync would conflict: `goal_envelope.py` fails a goal-authored change to it; `version_bump.py` measures a goal-branch range from where it left `main` and asks no top-level move in goal mode (a pull request into or out of `goal/<id>`, a push to one, or a goal's merge into `main`); after `complete` the agent opens the release bump, a pull request into `main` that moves only that line.
2. The runner counts every model call in a goal run. The dispatcher appends one line per call to the run's spend ledger, the file `GSTACK_GOAL_LEDGER` names: the provider, the cost the provider reported for that call, and the transport. A transport with no metered key (a subscription CLI), and a metered call whose provider reports no cost, count against Max calls, and `goal_brief.py check` refuses a dollar budget for a subscription CLI, which could never be reached. Before each step the runner reads the ledger with `goal_spend.py status`, stops at 80% of any provider's entry in Provider budgets, of the Spend cap, or of Max calls, and escalates (GO-006). A charge to a provider with no budget, a ledger it can't read, or a ledger write that fails stops the run. The run record shows the totals. The provider's own credit limit on the team key stays the backstop outside the agent.
3. `goals/<id>/HALT`, when present on `main`, stops the runner before the next step. Only a person can add it to `main`, because `goals/` is a `CODEOWNERS` path.
4. Rolling back a merged goal is a revert pull request of its merge commit through the normal gated path, never a reset of `main`. The final digest names the merge commit and the command that opens that revert.
5. Tests cover: a changed file outside Allowed paths; a changed `CODEOWNERS` path; a brief edited on the branch (ignored, `main`'s copy used); `main` advancing with a change to a `CODEOWNERS` path, synced into `goal/<id>` through the sync pull request, which passes the envelope check, while the same change authored on an item branch fails; a run at Max calls (stopped); Provider budgets that sum past the Spend cap (refused by `check`); spend split across two providers, each stopping at its own budget; a call whose provider reports no cost (counted against Max calls); a charge to a provider with no budget (stopped); an unreadable ledger (stopped); a ledger write that fails (raised past the dispatcher's own error handling); a halt file present (stopped).
6. Cumulative review and time limits (accepted 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`). Per delivered change, one initial independent review and at most two repair-verification passes in total, counting replacement review IDs; a fourth reservation is refused. Recovery stops after two repair cycles for a recurring failing check without a resolved cause. Approved active-time limits persist across restarts and are not reset by a new session, branch, item name or review ID; waiting on CI or an owner is reported separately with a bounded deadline. Failed dispatches and calls count under the existing spend policy. An unreadable gate, budget or authority record blocks further action and is never read as a pass.

### GO-005 — A judgment call is a record, and code decides who makes it

**Status:** done (PRs #107 and #108). Criteria 1 to 3 are in `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/goal_calls.py` and `goal_run.py`'s `call`, `council` and `answer` (PR #107); criteria 4 and 5 in `goal_guard.py` and `goal_run.py`'s `act` and `may`, with criterion 6's tests in `test_goal_guard.py`.

**Readings recorded:** item branches are `build/*`, the names `/gstack-build` gives them. "Deployment integrations the repository reports" are the environments GitHub lists and those its deployments name, so an app that deploys on a push without reporting an environment isn't seen. A workflow counts as triggering when any of `push`, `pull_request`, `pull_request_target`, `workflow_run` or `merge_group` appears in it (refusing on the safe side). The repository's own checks are `tests.yml` and the three goal workflows at the content pinned in `goal_guard.CHECKS`, which moves only in a change Dorian approves; a test fails CI while a pin and its workflow disagree. The brief's Stop conditions can name only an environment or a workflow whose top-level `on:` block is exactly `push` with `branches: [main]`, comments aside. An item merge also needs `tests`, `goal-envelope` and `goal-tests` green at its head, read from GitHub's check runs, and the goal checks count only when their newest run's `external_id` names this pull request, the goal branch and its current base. The recheck reads the workflows on `main`, because the envelope keeps `.github/` out of every goal change. An item merge reads its review record from `GSTACK_PEER_REVIEW_DIR`. On a private repository the goal-run token can't list environments, which needs Actions read, so `start` refuses there until the app and the goal-run token carry it (review F3, deferred; this repository is public).

**Links introduced:** the decision-to-rule link. The action type a decision record names is what the change does as far as the code detectors in criterion 2 reach, and anything they can't place goes to Dorian.

1. Every judgment call the agent makes during a run is written by code to the run record: the question, the options, who framed them, the action type, the votes and dissent, the commit IDs it affects.
2. Action types are a fixed list, and code assigns them from the change, not from the agent's label: `in_envelope_code` (every file inside Allowed paths, no change to a dependency manifest or lockfile, no new environment variable name, no new hostname in code or config), `dependency` (a manifest or lockfile changed), `external` (a new hostname or environment variable name), `goal_brief` (anything under `goals/`), and `unclassified` (anything else).
3. Only `in_envelope_code` is decided by the council: two models from families other than the builder's, one told to argue against the leading option, with the options framed by a model other than the one proposing. A split council escalates. Every other type goes to Dorian, and the run waits.
4. Every action the runner or a builder executes during a goal run is also checked against the existing action classes in `governance.py` (`GRANT_CLASSES`, `ONE_SHOT_ONLY`, `check_egress`) with a ledger made for the goal. That ledger grants `vcs.push` to `goal/<id>` and item branches, `pr.open` into `goal/<id>` and once from `goal/<id>` into `main`, `review.send_code` and `external.model_call` to the reviewers the run uses, and `net.connect` to their hosts; it never grants `merge`, which stays in `ONE_SHOT_ONLY`. An item pull request merges into `goal/<id>` under DR-113's authority, exactly as a change merges into `main`: pinned to its head, after a clean cross-vendor review at that head with coverage `checked` and the goal checks green, recorded by `record-release`. That authority covers `goal/<id>` targets only; the final merge into `main` follows GO-003.7. Any other action, including one that reaches an external system through a hostname or variable that already exists, is refused at the agent's step and sent to Dorian. That refusal is attention, not containment (*Constraints*). Containment is GitHub's: the `goal/*` and `main` rulesets, the app's permissions, and criterion 5. An `in_envelope_code` type never authorizes an action the ledger doesn't grant.
5. Pushes and merges are assessed for their effects before a run starts. `goal_runner.py` refuses to start when any workflow under `.github/workflows/`, or any deployment integration the repository reports, triggers on a push to `goal/*` or item branches, on a pull request into `goal/*` or `main`, or on a push to `main`, and does anything beyond the repository's own checks and the goal checks, and it names each one. A goal whose merge into `main` would trigger a deployment needs that deployment named in its brief's Stop conditions as Dorian's call, or the run doesn't start. The runner repeats this check against the current workflows and integrations before every push, pull request it opens and merge, and stops if anything it would trigger differs from the start of the run.
6. Tests cover: an in-envelope call recorded and decided; a workflow that deploys on a push to any branch, and one that deploys on a pull request, each of which stops the run from starting; a deploying workflow added to `main` after the run started, which stops the run before its next push; an item merge into `goal/<id>` after a clean review, allowed, and the same path aimed at `main`, refused; the final pull request into `main` opened under the one-time grant, and a second one refused; a call to an existing external host that isn't a reviewer, refused by the ledger although the change is typed `in_envelope_code`; a lockfile change, a new hostname and a new environment variable name each typed by code and sent to Dorian whatever the agent labels them; an unclassified call that waits; a split council that escalates.
7. The repository's own checks are pinned in that repository (amended 2026-10-05). The three goal workflows are the same file in every governed repository, so `goal_guard.GOAL_WORKFLOWS` pins them. Each repository pins its own checks, its `tests` workflow first, in `.github/goal-checks.json` on its `main` as `{"checks": {"<workflow file>": "<sha256>"}}`. That's a `CODEOWNERS` path, so a pin moves only in a change Dorian approves. A pin file that doesn't parse, pins a goal workflow, or pins anything but a workflow file to a lowercase SHA-256 is a problem that stops the run. A test fails CI while any pin and its workflow disagree.
8. A preview deploy that a hosting integration builds on every pushed branch (Vercel's, say) is outside what GitHub reports, so criterion 5 can't see it. It's allowed only when the project's Preview environment variables hold no production credential: test or sandbox values only, so code that hasn't been reviewed yet has nothing worth taking. Turning previews off isn't required. `/init-goal-mode` says so for any repository with such an integration. Before the first goal there, Dorian confirms it, or the agent establishes it from the host's API without reading a value. Until one of those happens it's reported unverified, and no goal runs there (amended 2026-10-05).

### GO-006 — Dorian hears from the run at milestones and on escalation, and nothing else

**Status:** review; human merge pending. Built in `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/goal_run.py` (`notify`, `digest`, `escalate`, `outbox`, `acknowledge`, `holdout-failed`, `ack`) with criterion 4's tests in `test_goal_digest.py`.

**Readings recorded:** "sends" means the runner prints each new message with whatever command produced it and the agent relays it to Dorian as written; the run record keeps it whether or not he saw it. Findings carry no category, so code reads "the same finding category" as a blocking finding in the same file, by its bound path in the review record, on two consecutive items. "What the agent is least sure of" is the agent's own statement, required on every `merged`. A close call is one with dissent. The escalations on the review cap, spend, a goal-test regression and a holdout failure end the run; one on a call that's Dorian's waits through the call; a goal-ledger refusal, a resync the envelope refuses and the repeated-file escalation hold the run until he acknowledges it in his words, and every action refuses while one is open. Spend is checked before every action as well as between items. An item pull request that `goal-envelope` refuses is criterion 2's change outside the envelope: `goal_run.py refused` escalates it with the check's own output quoted, and it holds the run like a goal-ledger refusal (added after PR #131, the adversarial pilot's scripted attempt, was refused by the check with no path into the run record).

1. The runner sends one digest per finished item and one at the end, answering three questions: which calls were close, what the agent is least sure of, and what changed against the brief. Dissent is shown by default. A digest asks nothing of him and gates nothing.
2. It escalates at once on: the review cap reached on an item, 80% of the Spend cap or Max calls, a change outside the envelope, the same finding category on two consecutive items, a goal-test regression, a holdout failure, or a GO-005 call that's Dorian's. An escalation waits for him.
3. Every digest and escalation is written into the run record as well as sent, so a missed message is still on file.
4. A test covers each escalation trigger in criterion 2 with a stub run, and checks that a digest doesn't block the next item.
5. In delivery mode the run escalates only for a changed outcome or scope, missing authority or access, exhausted limits, a mandatory owner gate, or a substantive blocker with no allowed recovery (accepted 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`). It never asks whether to continue an already authorized step, and sends one consolidated report per escalation: intended outcome, exact blocker and evidence, recovery attempted, remaining budget, smallest decision needed.

### GO-007 — `/gstack-goal-new` drafts a goal, and no one family writes the tests, explains them and builds against them

**Status:** review. Opened 2026-10-04.

**Links introduced:** none. The drafter, the reader and the builder were already three families (GO-002.1, GO-002.4, GO-003.3); this item makes the drafting path one command.

1. `/gstack-goal-new <id> "<outcome>"` drafts `goals/<id>/` on a branch from `origin/main`. The agent writes `GOAL.md` and `PLAN.md`. It agrees the outcome with Dorian in one exchange and asks him nothing else before the pull request.
2. `goal_new.py tests <goal dir>` has the gpt family, through Codex read-only, draft exactly the test files the brief's Goal tests name. Each file carries that family's `drafted-by` line, whatever line the drafter wrote. A reply missing a named file, or adding one, writes nothing and exits 1. A drafted test is never edited by hand: a wrong one is fixed in the brief or plan and drafted again.
3. `goal_new.py holdout <goal dir>` has the same family draft the holdout against the plan and the pre-mortem. The holdout goes to `~/.goal-holdouts/<id>.py` (folder 700, file 600; `GSTACK_GOAL_HOLDOUTS` overrides), refused when that folder is inside the repository. Only its SHA-256 enters `holdout.sha256`. The command prints the file's path, the secret name `GOAL_<ID>_HOLDOUT` and the environment, never the holdout. A reply that doesn't parse or has no `class Holdout` writes nothing and exits 1. It stays there until the run's finalize step carries it into the goal pull request (GO-002.8).
4. `goal_new.py read <goal dir>` has the gemini family, through OpenRouter, read every goal test back in plain English. It prints the goal pull request's `## Plain-English reading` section with its `Read by:` line. It refuses a reading that misses a test, a model below the reader's floor, and tests whose drafting family is missing or is the reader's.
5. `goal_brief.py check` and `baseline` still decide whether the folder is a goal. The command opens the pull request adding `goals/<id>/` alone and tells Dorian, in one message, the link, that it needs his approval, and where the holdout secret goes.
6. A test covers, with stub drafters: tests written under the drafter's family; a missing file refused with nothing written; a brief with no goal tests; the holdout outside the repository at 600, hashed, its source absent from the output; a holdout folder inside the repository refused; an unparseable holdout and one with no class refused; a reading covering every test with its reader named; a reading that misses a test, one below the floor, and tests drafted by the reader's family, each refused.
7. product-orchestrator's sprint protocol routes a build plan of two or more items to `/gstack-goal-new`, then `/gstack-goal` once Dorian approves the goal, and a single item to `/gstack-build`. It names `/init-goal-mode` as the step that says whether a repository can run goals (amended 2026-10-04).

### GO-008 — Before goal mode runs in a repository, a check says what that repository lacks

**Status:** review. Opened 2026-10-04.

Everything that enforces goal mode lives in the governed repository's own `main` under Dorian's code-owner review. Another repository needs the same pieces before `/gstack-goal` can run there, and this check says which it has.

**Links introduced:** none. The check reads; it changes nothing in either repository.

1. `goal_ready.py <owner>/<repo>`, run from a moxywolf-plugins checkout under `agent_token.py exec --repo <owner>/<repo>`, reads the repository through the GitHub API and reports seven requirements: the bot can see it, read its check runs and its deployments, and its default branch is `main`; `DESIGN.md` on `main` has at least one objective heading; `CODEOWNERS` names Dorian on every gate path this repository's `CODEOWNERS` lists under `/.github/`, `/goals/`, `/goal-runs/` and the goal-mode scripts; `.github/goal-checks.json` pins the repository's own checks, and they and the three goal workflows match their pins byte for byte (GO-005.7); every non-test file under `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/` is the same git blob as the checkout's `HEAD`; `main`'s rules require code-owner review and the `tests`, `goal-envelope`, `goal-tests` and `goal-holdout` checks, and block force pushes and deletion; the `goal-holdout` environment deploys from `main` only.
2. Each requirement prints `ready`, `missing` or `unknown` (the API refused) with what it examined, then a count line. It exits 0 only when all seven are ready. Unknown is not ready. A repository the token can't see stops after its first line.
3. `/init-goal-mode <owner>/<repo>` in project-init runs the check and turns each gap into who acts. Dorian installs the app, sets the ruleset and the environment, and approves any `CODEOWNERS` change. The agent writes `DESIGN.md` through `/gstack-design-doc`. It changes nothing on its own.
4. Installing goal mode in another repository is not built here, and the check says so by its own result. The per-repository pin is GO-005.7 (2026-10-05). The sandbox runs a Node candidate as GO-002.7 sets out (2026-10-05). Copying the workflows and scripts into a repository is that repository's own onboarding pull request, which Dorian approves; it's never done by hand.
5. A test covers, against a stub API built from this checkout: a repository set up like this one, ready on all seven; each requirement failing alone; a refused call reported as unknown; an invisible repository stopping after one line.

### GO-010 — A goal runs in any repository, with no onboarding

**Status:** building. Declared 2026-10-05 on Dorian's instruction, after a goal on SAMS was refused for lacking setup: "Fix it NOW so that I can test it on ANY ... REPO".

**Links introduced:** none. Results print as JSON and go into the goal pull request and the run's RESULT.md.

In local mode, for a repository whose own `main` doesn't carry the gates (workflows, gate CODEOWNERS, a ruleset, an environment), the checks GitHub would run in CI run on the Release Owner's Mac, in the same containers, from the moxywolf-plugins checkout, and Dorian's approvals of the goal pull request and the final pull request are the gate. In such a repository GitHub enforces nothing extra, which the pull request says.

1. `goal_local.py check <repo-dir> <id> <head> [--holdout <file>] [--goal-dir <dir>]`, run from a moxywolf-plugins checkout, runs every goal test against `<head>` in the goal-tests container with the verdict scripts from that checkout, never from the repository under test, and with the goal folder from the repository's `origin/main` or `--goal-dir`. With `--holdout` it checks the file against `holdout.sha256` and runs it in the holdout container. It prints one JSON object and exits 0 only when every goal test, the holdout when given and the envelope passed.
2. A pnpm workspace installs in its own networked container from its manifests only (every tracked `package.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, `.npmrc`), frozen lockfile, scripts off, hoisted, with workspace links re-pointed at the sandbox's copy of the candidate. A symlinked manifest is refused. An npm repository uses the existing install.
3. The envelope applies one rule in every repository: it refuses any goal commit touching `.github/`, `goals/` or `goal-runs/`, any path the repository's own CODEOWNERS assigns an owner, or any file outside the brief's Allowed paths, and any change to the marketplace's top-level version. No CODEOWNERS file, complete or partial, switches the fixed gate off. Local mode commits no holdout, so there is no finalize commit to except.
4. `goal_local.py baseline <repo-dir> <id> --goal-dir <dir>` fails unless every invariant passes and every outcome test fails on `origin/main`, and `goal_local.py brief <repo-dir> <id>` runs `goal_brief.py check` with that repository's DESIGN.md and CODEOWNERS.
5. `/gstack-goal-new` and `/gstack-goal` describe local mode as the path for a repository `goal_ready.py` reports as not set up, asking Dorian for nothing beyond the goal approval and the final approval.
6. `test_goal_local.py` covers the local envelope (fixed gate, an ordinary or sentinel-only CODEOWNERS, the marketplace version), the manifest selection with a symlink refused, a dangling lockfile link, the link re-pointing, a baseline outcome that didn't run, and a failed check still printing JSON. The SAMS run is recorded in `docs/evidence/go-010-sams-2026-10-05.md`: its pnpm install, two of its unit test files passing in the sandbox, and a baseline with the invariant passing and the outcome failing.
7. `plugins/gstack-execution/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` moves to 0.77.0 and the marketplace to 1.115.0.

### GO-011 — Dorian co-authors the goal, the goal merges itself, and he tests usability before Production

**Status:** building. Declared 2026-10-24 on Dorian's instruction: "I want to set the goal. Co-Author the PR. And then once everything has been fully committed and e2e tests are done, I want to do usability tests." His answers the same day: the brief is co-authored as a live doc before its pull request; the usability test sits before Production, not before `main`; it's a guided task sheet; and the agent triages what he finds. His decision replaced the staged approvals in this objective's introduction.

**Links introduced:** a live doc per goal draft (Claude Docs) and a usability sheet per finished goal (an Artifact page with a shared store); both go to Dorian in the conversation, and their contents also land in `goal-runs/<id>/`. Criterion 9 adds two: the successor link, a `Succeeds <predecessor>:` paragraph in a successor's GOAL.md that `goal_successor.py` and `goal_brief.py verify` read to find the brief it must match; and the inherited approval, `approval_pr` and `approval_head` in a successor run's state and delivery record, naming the root goal's approving review that `establish_authority` re-reads from GitHub.

Dorian decides three things per goal: its outcome (co-authoring the brief), its approval (the goal pull request) and whether it's usable (the sheet, before Production). The agent asks him nothing else: no item, sync, finalize or final pull request waits on him while the whole-goal review's lane is wide; a closed lane waits for his merge-instruction on the final pull request (GA-011). A successor goal (criterion 9) asks him nothing to start or run; its merged result comes to him for the usability sheet and Production (criteria 4 to 6) as for any goal. These still reach him: the safety stops GO-005 and GO-006 define (spend at its stop, a change outside the envelope, a judgment call the council splits on or that isn't in-envelope code), which hold the run; a ledger refusal, for his attention (the ledger is attention, not containment: XE-002.6); an item whose review rounds run out, which ends the run (`goal_run.py failed`); and criterion 2's two merge asks (his merge-instruction on a closed lane, GA-011, and his recorded exception for a whole-goal review whose coverage isn't `checked`). When one means the goal is wrong, the agent proposes the next goal rather than an override.

1. `/gstack-goal-new` drafts the brief as a live doc Dorian can edit and comment on before anything is committed: his outcome in his words, the never-do's, the scenarios and the plan items. It opens the goal pull request only after he says the draft is right. The commit carries `Co-Authored-By: Dorian C <dorianc@moxywolf.com>`, and the pull request says the brief is co-authored. The tests, holdout and reading are still drafted by other families (GO-002, GO-007). His approving review of that pull request starts the run; a successor's run starts on its root goal's approval instead (criterion 9).
2. The goal branch's pull request into `main` merges as the bot, with no review by Dorian, while its lane is wide (a closed lane also needs his merge-instruction, GA-011), once `goal-envelope`, `goal-tests`, `goal-holdout` and `tests` are green and a fresh whole-goal cross-vendor review with coverage `checked` has no blocking findings (a review whose coverage isn't `checked` waits for his recorded exception and his merge, *Coverage is mandatory*). `CODEOWNERS` keeps `goal-runs/README.md` and stops owning a goal's own `goal-runs/<id>/`. The holdout is still checked against `goals/<id>/holdout.sha256`, which stays owned for every goal but a successor. A successor's hash sits in its unowned folder (criterion 9): its holdout is drafted by `goal_new.py holdout` exactly as a root goal's is, and his review of a root goal's hash never saw the holdout either (GO-002.8), so what the hash binds is unchanged. `goal_run.py propose` says so in the pull request, and `goal_run.py may --action merge --resource main` grants the merge only then: the review pinned to the head where the goal tests passed, or after a sync from `main` to a fresh review of the new head without its holdout. The review's packet (`packet.json`, which `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/peer_review.py` writes at `open` with `review_base` set to the base and keeps through fix rounds) names every item and starts where `main` and the goal head meet.
3. A failed `goal-holdout` never comes to Dorian as an override. The run ends, and the next goal follows with what failed as its input: a successor under criterion 9 when the approved brief can stay as it is, otherwise a goal the agent drafts with him (criterion 1). A whole-goal review that blocks after finalize ends the run the same way (`goal_run.py review-failed`), since a fix would move the head the goal tests and the holdout passed at.
4. After the goal merges, `/gstack-usability <id>` writes a usability sheet: the Preview of the merged commit, and five to eight tasks taken from the goal's Scenarios, each written as something a person does ("find which skills automate incident response"). For each task Dorian marks worked, confusing or broken and can add a note; the sheet stores his answers where the agent reads them back. The agent sends him the link once and doesn't nag.
5. The agent triages his answers. Wording, layout, styling, a broken link or an accessibility fix that doesn't change what the product does is fixed through `/gstack-build` (cross-vendor review, no goal), verified on the Preview, and handed back as a re-test sheet with only those tasks. Anything that changes what the product does becomes the next goal, drafted with him (criterion 1).
6. Production means the product's own domain (for wfa-web, wfa.opencontrols.ai). A host that deploys on every merge into `main` (Vercel's Git integration) also serves the host's own address (on Vercel, `<project>-<suffix>.vercel.app`, which Vercel leaves public); that isn't Production. Dorian decided both on 2026-10-24: "keep as is" over a release branch or testing before the merge, and "leave it public" for the vercel.app address. The brief's Stop conditions name attaching the domain as his call, and `/gstack-usability` checks the domain isn't attached before sending the sheet. This covers a product's first attach. A repository whose domain is already attached, where every merge into `main` is a Production deploy, isn't settled here: its goal names the order of the sheet and the merge in its Stop conditions, which Dorian co-authors. Production and domains stay his call, asked once, when every task's latest mark across the goal's sheets is worked (unanswered isn't, and a task sent to the next goal keeps its mark) or he says to ship. Nothing else about the goal comes to him beyond the stops and asks this item's introduction names.
7. `plugins/gstack-execution/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` moved to 0.93.0 and the marketplace to 1.147.0 with this item (applied; later criteria move them from where they stand then).
8. The goal approval carries goal-level merge authority (accepted 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`, in the approving person's own words). It covers implementation, verification, review, routine in-scope fixes, branch pushes, PR creation and gated merges for that goal, including its declared release-companion PR into `main`, which the runner tracks, reviews, merges as the app and records before declaring the goal complete; inside those boundaries the workflow asks for no per-PR merge permission. It waives nothing: CODEOWNERS, repository rules, deployment restrictions, data-use permissions and any separate required human approval still block the relevant action until satisfied. Scoped, freshness-checked context from the graph-context runtime (`graph_context.py`, attest, pointer bindings, preflight refusal) is how read-only graph workers receive evidence; no worker receives merge authority.
9. A successor goal needs no new approval (Dorian, 2026-11-15, after goal `review-packet-ready-6`'s whole-goal review blocked once the holdout had passed: "I approve this and it needs to be added to our plugins. Me manually going in stops things if I'm not at the desk."). When a goal's holdout fails or its whole-goal review blocks, the agent drafts `goals/<root>--s1/` (after `--s1`, `--s2`) with `goal_new.py successor`. GOAL.md is the predecessor's word for word plus one paragraph after the Outcome starting `Succeeds <predecessor>:`, except that Scenarios, Goal tests and Pre-mortem may change. PLAN.md (not compared) and the holdout are new, and the tests and holdout are drafted and read by the other families as for any goal (GO-002, GO-007). `goal_successor.py check`, a step of the required `tests` check on every pull request, refuses a successor folder that: lacks the predecessor's sections, each once and in the same order, each byte for byte but Scenarios, Goal tests and Pre-mortem (so Non-goals, Allowed paths, the budgets and limits, Stop conditions, Release companion and Required checkouts can't move); adds anything else to the Outcome; already existed; or arrives with a change outside its folder. It examines every folder name CODEOWNERS leaves unowned and refuses one that isn't a valid successor id. `CODEOWNERS` leaves `goals/*--s1/` and `goals/*--s2/` unowned and owns `goal_successor.py`, so no review of his is waited on. `goal_brief.py verify` accepts the root goal's approval for a successor after comparing the briefs again from `main`, and the run's delivery record binds that approval. The successor's pull request merges as the bot like any other change, on a clean cross-vendor review at its head and `tests` green there (`goal_successor.py` included), which takes the place of his approving review. Dorian can still stop one: his changes-requested review on its pull request makes `goal_brief.py verify` refuse it, so its run never starts. When `--s2` fails, the run ends and the next goal is his (a third successor, `--s3`, is owned like any goal folder); a fix that needs a kept section changed is likewise a new goal drafted with him. `plugins/gstack-execution` moves from 0.101.6 to 0.102.0 and the marketplace from 1.167.0 to 1.168.0.

### Dissent from the design review

Removed history (review narrative and evidence) is kept in [`DESIGN.md` at `ef74ce8`](https://github.com/MoxyWolfLLC/moxywolf-plugins/blob/ef74ce8/DESIGN.md).

Design review 2026-10-01 (XE-031's first live run: Codex on gpt-6-astra and Gemini 3.8 Flash, writer Claude): stopped at its five-round cap; all 36 findings accepted and fixed, the last two (`R5-r1-1` RESULT.md finalize path, `R5-r1-2` check-run binding to the merge candidate) applied after the last round and since built in GO-003.5 to .7; log `Taskade/Team Plugins/06 – Engineering/DESIGN-REVIEW-LOG-2026-10-01.md`.

### GO-012 — One release relationship, and the delivery record is written by the run that uses it

**Status:** building. Approved by Dorian 2026-10-27 ("Approve GO-012 as drafted"); drafted 2026-10-27 from `/gstack-investigate` on the delivery path at `5b1a6d8` (PR #267), after goals `gstack-delivery-through-merge` 1 to 5 and `gstack-delivery-completion` 1 to 3 each failed their own review on it.

**Links introduced:** the delivery-record link (`goal-runs/<id>/state.json` `delivery`, written by `goal_run.py start` and read by every later phase); the release-relationship link (one function, `goal_guard.release_relationship`, that every merge-shaped decision calls, so the recorded finalized head means the same thing in `may merge`, `next_action`, `complete` and `record-release`).

**Root cause (two findings, both confirmed in the code at `5b1a6d8`).**

- **Nothing creates the delivery record.** `state["delivery"]` is assigned only inside `goal_run.resume` (goal_run.py:1097, :1132), and `resume` refuses when the record is absent (:1063). `start`, `finalize`, `propose` and `complete` never write it. So in a real run `complete` reaches line 763 and ends the goal `complete` at the main merge, before the release record, the companion and the Mac sync. The end-to-end demonstration passed only because `test_goal_run.delivery_state` writes the record by hand (test_goal_run.py:524-540). GO-003.12 is therefore unbuilt behind a passing test (EV-001: a check over input it did not examine).
- **The reviewed-head/merged-head relationship has five homes:** `goal_guard.final_merge_allowed` (:208-214), `goal_run.final_review_pinned` (:418), `goal_run.complete` (:747), `goal_delivery.next_action` (:202-214, "Repair 6") and `peer_review.cmd_record_release` (`finalized_relation`, "Repair 6" again). Each round found two disagreeing and each repair added another home. In the chain from `87e7b81` to `5b1a6d8` (46 commits, 8 repair commits), these three files grew by 1,056 lines and 16 were removed. This is XE-008 ("a fact with two homes drifts") in the goal layer.

**Why a goal cannot fix it.** A goal run merges through this path, so a defect in the path blocks its own delivery. The change below is one ordinary `/gstack-build` change to `main` with one cross-vendor review, under the existing DR-113 rule; none of the files is a `CODEOWNERS` path except where noted.

1. `goal_run.py start` writes `state["delivery"]` (schema_version 1) from the approval it already verifies: `approval` = the goal PR, Dorian's approving review id, its commit id and his login; `max_review_rounds` = the brief's Max review rounds; `changes` = `final` (target `main`, repository from the checkout's origin) and, when the brief declares one under `## Release companion`, the companion (target `main`); plan items are not duplicated here, because the run already keeps them in `items`, `done` and `results` and the whole-goal review scope checks them (amended 2026-10-27 with Dorian's approval, before any code); `release_companion` from `## Release companion` (`marketplace` or `none`) and `required_checkouts` from `## Required checkouts` (one line each: `name path host branch`), both empty when the brief has no such section. `resume` on a run started before this change still refuses with its current message.
2. Each existing phase binds its own facts into the record as it goes: `finalized_head` and `final_pr` in `propose`; the whole-goal review id at `may merge main`; the merge sha from GitHub's observation in `resume`. No new phase and no second place for any of these; the record references the existing run fields and review ids.
3. One function, `goal_guard.release_relationship(state, head, pr, pinned, scope_problems)`, returns `{"ok": bool, "finalized_head", "tested_head", "problems"}`: the pull request is the recorded goal pull request, its head is `state["finalized_head"]`, and the review is pinned to the tested head without its holdout and covers the whole goal (`goal_run.final_review_pinned` and `final_review_scope`, passed in because `goal_guard` can't import `goal_run`). `final_merge_allowed`, `goal_delivery.next_action` (the `ch["final"]` branch), `goal_run.complete` and `peer_review.cmd_record_release` call it and keep no comparison of their own. A test asserts by source that the strings `finalized_head` and `tested head` are compared in that one function only.
4. `goal_run.py complete` no longer ends the run when a delivery record exists: it records the merge and returns the delivery status, and `resume` (or the same session continuing) carries the release record, the companion and the sync to `complete`. Only `resume`'s end at `delivery_status == "complete"` writes outcome `complete`.
5. The four blockers from review `20261027-1549…` of `5b1a6d8` are folded in, from the desktop session's unpushed `e9a3055` where they match this design: a release re-check compares like rounds only; an item review is never accepted as the whole-goal review (`final_review_scope` is called from `release_relationship`); a checkout's repository comes from its remote, and a declared `repo` that disagrees blocks; a Git read that times out reports `sync_pending`.
6. The end-to-end demonstration test starts from `goal_run.start` with no hand-written delivery record and runs finalize, propose, merge, record-release, companion and sync against the fixture GitHub. On the tree before this change it fails as soon as `start` has run, because `start` writes no record (`KeyError: 'delivery'` at the first read of it; the run would otherwise reach `complete` and end there with nothing delivered), and it passes with this change (amended 2026-10-28 under Dorian's pre-approval of 2026-10-27 23:09 PT: "I pre-approve all work that will get this done").
7. `test_goal_run` keeps a case that a record `resume` would accept is refused when `start` did not write it.
8. gstack-execution gets a minor bump and a README Version History line; the marketplace entry and top-level version move (CI-002).

**Not in this item.** The fourteen embedded-instruction follow-ups the reviews listed; the preserved candidate branches, which stay as they are; moving the review verdict into a GitHub-required check (named as later work in the DR-113 constraint).

**Evidence for the reviewer.** The two root-cause citations above, with line numbers at `5b1a6d8`; the chain statistics (`git diff --stat 87e7b81~1..5b1a6d8` on the three files; `git log --oneline 87e7b81..5b1a6d8 | grep -ciE 'repair|fix'` = 8 of 46); and the demonstration test's red run on `main` before the change.

### GO-017 — Independent items build in parallel and integrate in dependency order through one coordinator

**Status:** review. PR #354 merged to `main` as `ef74ce8` by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` (read from GitHub 2026-11-20); no `record-release` record verified. Declared 2026-11-19 at Dorian's instruction ("Make parallel builds a permanent capability ... The rule is: build and test independent work in parallel; integrate in dependency order through one coordinator ... Start with three concurrent workers and one coordinator"; GO-023, when built, removes the three-worker limit). Built on `build/GO-017-swarm`. Depends on MR-004 for worker launch: a worker's `model` comes from `route.py recommend`, never from a hook. Removed history (review narrative and evidence) is kept in [`DESIGN.md` at `ef74ce8`](https://github.com/MoxyWolfLLC/moxywolf-plugins/blob/ef74ce8/DESIGN.md).

**Links introduced:** `goals/<id>/DEPS.json` (optional, approved and frozen with the goal: `{version: 1, max_workers: 1-3 (changes under GO-023: optional, any positive integer), items: {"<PLAN item n>": {id, writes, start_after, integrate_after, contracts: [{path, sha256}], estimate_usd}}, order: [[a, b]]}`), read by `swarm.py load_plan` and by `goal_run.py start`; the coordinator's run state `<GSTACK_GOAL_RUN_DIR>/<id>/swarm/swarm.json` and `swarm-events.jsonl`, written only by `swarm.py`; `goal_run.py next`'s new step `{"step": "swarm", items, swarm_run, goal_dir, ...}`, read by `/gstack-goal`; state field `parallel` (`{"<n>": {id, integrate_after: [n]}}`), read by `current_item`.

goal_run built one item at a time: `next` returned `remaining[0]` and `merged` refused any other item. Items that share no files and no interface still waited for each other's builds, reviews and CI. The swarm keeps every gate a serial item passes and changes only what may run at the same time. PLAN.md's format is untouched (goal_brief.py checks it strictly and every approved goal relies on it), so the dependency declaration is a separate file; a goal without it is the serial chain it always was. A start dependency blocks dispatch until its prerequisite has merged. An integration dependency blocks only the merge, so a page can be drafted and tested against a pinned interface while the code behind the interface is still being built. The three boundary tests passed on 2026-09-29, so this graph work meets *Test the boundaries before adding item IDs*. Not taken: swarm workers for protected review or security work, until a refused protected launch is shown on a real host even when the model-router hook can't start (Dorian, 2026-11-19; the hook wiring is PR #355); more than three workers before three have run; workers that dispatch workers; a merge queue that merges for the coordinator; any change to the review cap, the holdout, CODEOWNERS or production authorization.

1. `swarm.py load_plan` reads `DEPS.json` and refuses: entries that don't match PLAN.md's numbered items, ids that aren't unique lowercase-hyphenated, a missing or self prerequisite, a cycle across start, integration and order edges, a contract not pinned as `{path, sha256}`, `max_workers` outside 1 to 3 (changes under GO-023: a `max_workers` that is not a positive integer), and two items whose writes overlap with no order between them, where any path of `start_after`, `integrate_after` or `order` edges is an order. With only an integration order, both may be drafted from the same base and the later one is refreshed onto the earlier one's merge (criterion 6). Without `DEPS.json` it returns a serial chain with one worker. `test_swarm.py` covers each refusal and the serial chain.
2. `tick` claims every eligible item up to `max_workers` (changes under GO-023: the smaller of the goal's `max_workers` and the host limit, where each applies, counting claimed slots) in one call, not just the first: an item is eligible when its start prerequisites have merged, no ancestor is blocked, no HALT or spend stop is in force, a worker is free and its estimate fits the budget left after spending and reservations. A claimed item that hasn't launched yet holds its worker slot. Each claim carries the item, attempt, pinned base (the plan base, or the integration head when it has start prerequisites), a branch of its own, its writable paths, its pinned contracts, what to return (the head it committed on the claim's branch or a patch against the pinned base, the tests it ran, unresolved issues) and what it may not do. A returned patch is applied by the coordinator with `swarm.py apply-patch`, which commits it on the claim's branch at the pinned base and prints the head; `result` then checks that head like any other. A worker blocked while it runs keeps its worker slot until it reports. `test_swarm.py` runs two controlled workers in their own worktrees on threads and asserts their spans overlap.
3. A claimed item is `dispatched` until `launched` records that the host accepted the call; `launch-failed` records the host's words, releases the reservation, and makes the item eligible again, blocked after two failed launches (counted on their own, not by attempt number). A refused launch never reads as running. A restarted coordinator that ticks again dispatches nothing already claimed and charges nothing twice; `result` settles each attempt once. The budget `init` takes is a **dispatch budget, not a spend cap** (Dorian, 2026-11-19): swarm workers are Agent-tool workers, the host gives the coordinator no way to stop one once it is launched, and it reports their usage in tokens, not dollars (measured 2026-11-19; a CLI worker's `--max-budget-usd` overshoots by up to one call and is not used here). What it guarantees, with workers already running: no item is dispatched unless its estimate fits the budget left after everything charged and everything reserved for work in flight; HALT or a spend stop dispatches nothing new. Because no worker's cost can be verified, a settled attempt is charged its full reservation, and a reported figure can only add to that (logged `overrun`), never release any of it; every settlement is logged `verified: false` and counted in `estimated_settlements`. A total charged above the budget is logged `over_budget` and shown by `status`, and from then on nothing is dispatched. When nothing is in flight, no money can come back (a reservation is released only by a failed launch or by a block before launch, criterion 5), so an eligible item whose estimate doesn't fit what's left is blocked as `dispatch budget exhausted`, its dependents with it, and the run can end through criterion 7's rule. What it does not guarantee: that a running worker stops, or that real spend stays under the budget. Every dollar value, a plan's `estimate_usd`, `--budget` and `--cost`, must be a finite number, not negative, and estimates and the budget above zero; anything else is refused (review 20261119-203612 F11). A goal that requires a hard dollar cap can't run as a swarm and is refused at start: a `DEPS.json` with `"hard_dollar_cap": true`, or a brief whose Spend cap section calls it hard. `DEPS.json` keys the coordinator doesn't read are refused, so no cap request is ignored. The goal's own spend ledger (`goal_spend.py`) and its 80% stop are unchanged and apply on top.
4. `result` diffs the worker's head against its claim's base, with rename detection off so both sides of a move count, and rejects a change outside its writable paths, a change to a pinned contract, failing tests or an empty change. Accepted evidence records head, base, files, tests and unresolved issues.
5. `review` counts rounds per item across attempts and refreshes and accepts only a review of the drafted head. Through review, only a blocking round can block: when a blocking round reaches the cap the item is blocked and is never redispatched, while a clean review, a re-review after a refresh included, never blocks. (Two failed launches, criterion 3, and a result outside the claim, criterion 4, block an item too.) So another item's merge can't block a clean item; it costs a review, not the cap. `blocked`, and every other way an item becomes blocked, stops the item and, at once, every unmerged item that depends on it through `start_after`, `integrate_after` or `order`, in flight or not, and nothing else. A blocked claim that never launched gives its reservation back; a running one keeps it until its worker reports, and that report settles the spend without advancing the item. The review cap is the brief's Max review rounds per item when the goal has a `GOAL.md`.
6. `merge-gate` reports a moved target for any item, and evidence not built on the target for any item with evidence, and passes only when the item is reviewed at the tested head, every prerequisite (start, integration and order) has merged, no HALT is in force, the target is still where the coordinator's last merge left it, and the reviewed head is built on the target. Otherwise it lists every reason. `refresh` records a head the coordinator rebuilt on the moved target, checks it against the claim from the target, drops the old review and keeps the round count; the item is reviewed again before merge. `merged` advances the integration head. The gate adds to the item merge's existing gates and replaces none: the merge still goes through `goal_run.py may ... merge`, which needs a clean cross-vendor review with coverage `checked` and green checks at that head, and a goal's change to a `CODEOWNERS` path is refused by `goal-envelope` at the item merge and at the goal's pull request into `main`, for a swarm item exactly as for a serial one (GO-004.1). Nothing here changes either, and a `DEPS.json` `writes` entry grants nothing.
7. `goal_run.py start` validates `DEPS.json` with `load_plan` and records `parallel`; `next` returns `{"step": "swarm"}` with every unmerged item after HALT, the changed-goal check, the spend stop, waiting calls and escalations that wait for Dorian, exactly as before (a `blocked` escalation is recorded and told to him but doesn't wait, so it never holds the swarm step); `merged` accepts any unmerged item whose prerequisites (start, integration and order) have merged and refuses the rest; `failed` accepts any unmerged item, so a run with a blocked item can always end. In a swarm, when an item is blocked the coordinator runs `goal_run.py blocked <id> --item N --reason R` at once: it escalates to Dorian (`review_cap` for an exhausted review, so three blocking rounds still reach him when they happen) without holding or ending the run, and is refused on a serial run. Independent items keep being dispatched, reviewed and merged. The coordinator calls `failed` only when nothing else can proceed: every unmerged item is blocked (an exhausted dispatch budget included, criterion 3) or waits on a blocked one. A `DEPS.json` in a successor folder (`goals/<root>--s1/`, `--s2/`) is refused at start (changes under GO-023 criterion 8: a successor may carry its predecessor's `DEPS.json` under the copy rule): `goal_successor.py` holds that unowned folder to the root's brief, not to a dependency plan, so a successor runs serially. `test_goal_run.py` passes unchanged for serial goals, and `ParallelPlan` covers the swarm step, merge order and an unordered overlap refused at start.
8. `/gstack-goal`, `/gstack-goal-new` and `/gstack-build` say how the coordinator drives `swarm.py`, how `DEPS.json` is drafted and checked, and what a worker may and may not do. This item moves gstack-execution to 0.106.0 (from 0.105.1) in `plugin.json`, its marketplace entry and the README version history, and moves the top-level marketplace version.
9. A real run on the host: three workers launched in one message, their start and end times recorded, every result checked by `result`, the items integrated in dependency order through `merge-gate`, and the run's events kept in `docs/pilots/GO-017-2026-11-19/`. A unit test alone doesn't satisfy this criterion.

### GO-023 — Every goal runs independent work in parallel, with no fixed worker cap, and says what must run in sequence

**Status:** building. Dorian's direction, 2026-11-19: "We also want to add that all goals will *always* run multiple agents in parallel", and, on a first-run cap of 2: "no. As many as necessary for the goal. Why limit it?" The mechanics below (no fixed cap, host back-off, waiting for a slot at the lower ceiling, a separately approved retest, `DEPS.json` rules for successors and hard-cap goals, the 3-against-30 proof on the Cowork host, and closing the launch gap) are Dex's recommendations of 2026-11-20, which Dorian passed on. Dorian's approval of this item is recorded in the Amendments log from the design page when he marks it approved; it covers GO-023 only, including the 30-worker proof in criterion 9, and builds no part of GO-018 to GO-022.

**Links introduced:** the host limit record `<GSTACK_GOAL_RUN_DIR>/hosts/<host>.json` (`{limit, events: [{run, kind: "refused" | "retest", running, approval, at}]}`), written only by `swarm.py`, shared by every run on that host and updated atomically under a lock, so two coordinators on one host never overwrite a lower limit or consume the same retest approval twice. A reader of a host's limit assumes the host either refused a launch above it or carried that many in an approved retest with no refusal. That holds because only a refused launch lowers it, and only such a retest raises it.

**Supersedes in GO-017.** These GO-017 phrases stop binding when this item merges, and each is amended in place to point here: the Links line's `max_workers: 1-3` (now optional, a positive integer with no upper bound); criterion 1's refusal of "`max_workers` outside 1 to 3" (now only a non-positive or non-integer value is refused); criterion 2's "up to `max_workers`" (now the smaller of the goal's `max_workers`, if any, and the host limit, if any). GO-017's Status line quotes Dorian's "Start with three concurrent workers and one coordinator"; the quote stays as history and no longer sets a limit. GO-017 criterion 9 (the three-worker run on 2026-11-19) is a record of that proof, not a limit, and stays. GO-017 criterion 7's refusal of a `DEPS.json` in a successor folder is superseded by criterion 8 here: a successor may carry its predecessor's `DEPS.json` under the copy rule. GO-017's serial chain for a goal with no `DEPS.json` stays, for goals approved before this item (criterion 8). GO-017's two hard-cap markers stay in force (criterion 8).

1. No fixed worker cap. `swarm.py` has no `MAX_WORKERS` constant and no upper bound on `max_workers`. `max_workers` is optional in `DEPS.json`: when present it is a positive integer and the goal's own ceiling; when absent the goal has no ceiling of its own. `grep -rn 'MAX_WORKERS\|1-3\|1 to 3\|no more than 3' plugins/gstack-execution --include=*.py --include=*.md` (tests excluded) prints no line about a worker cap (README Version History lines and unrelated limits such as `max_attempts` are not worker caps). A test covers `max_workers` absent (accepted, no ceiling), 40 (accepted), and 0 and `"3"` (refused).
2. Default dispatch. `swarm.py tick` claims every ready item: its `start_after` items have merged, its `writes` don't overlap a running item's, the dispatch budget (GO-017) covers its estimate, and the count of running and claimed items stays at or below the smaller of the goal's `max_workers` and the host limit, where each applies; in a run started with a verified retest (criterion 5) the approved retest ceiling stands in for the host limit until the run ends or a refusal comes (a claim holds a slot from the tick that makes it, GO-017). Write overlap is checked against running items and against items claimed earlier in the same tick. When only one item can run, it runs one. A test covers 40 ready independent items with no ceilings (40 claimed in one tick), a goal `max_workers` of 5 (5 claimed), a host limit of 12 (12 claimed), and two ready items with overlapping writes in one tick (one claimed).
3. Launch check. `tick` and `launch-check` use the same effective ceiling. The approved retest ceiling replaces the host limit only while a verified retest is open and no host capacity refusal has occurred in that run. Otherwise, both use the current host limit. Immediately before launching a claimed item, the coordinator runs `swarm.py launch-check <run> <id> --attempt N --halt <path>`. It re-reads HALT, the spend stop and the host limit, and counts launched and claimed slots. When HALT or the spend stop is in force, or the item's slot is above the current host limit, it returns the claim to pending, releases its reservation uncharged, and prints `do not launch`. After a host refusal (criterion 4), every claim not yet launched that is now above the lower limit returns to pending the same way. The coordinator sends launches in one message only for claims that passed the check; a refusal inside one message cannot stop calls already sent in it, so each of those is recorded as `launched` or `launch-failed` from the host's own answer. A launch that was sent and not yet acknowledged is in flight: it keeps its slot and its reservation through a HALT, a spend stop, a host refusal and a coordinator restart, until the host's answer reconciles it (`launched`, or `launch-failed` and released). `launch-check`, the back-off and `tick` after a restart never release it or claim its slot for another item. A test covers 30 claims with a refusal at the 15th launch: the 14 launched before it stay running, claims not yet launched above the new limit return to pending with no charge, and no further item passes the check until a slot frees; and a HALT raised after a tick stops every claim not yet launched. A test also covers a launch sent and unacknowledged when HALT is raised, and again when the coordinator restarts: its slot and reservation stay held, no other item takes the slot, and both are released or settled only when `launched` or `launch-failed` records the host's answer.
4. Back-off on a host refusal. `swarm.py launch-failed <run> <id> --attempt N --reason R --host-refused` records a launch the host refused for capacity. It does not count toward the item's two-failure block (GO-017). The host limit drops to the number of workers running when the refusal came, and during a retest to the smaller of the current limit and that number, so a refusal never raises a limit (launched workers plus launches sent and not yet acknowledged; claims not yet sent don't count and go back to pending), never below 1, and the item returns to pending. `tick` does not claim it again until fewer workers are running than the new limit, so it waits for a slot at the lower ceiling and is not relaunched in a loop. Each refusal lowers the limit or holds it at 1, so refusals can't repeat without end: a refusal at a limit of 1 counts toward the item's block as any failed launch does. A refused launch's reservation is released uncharged, since nothing ran. When every claim of one message was sent, all of them count as sent; none is "not yet launched", and the new limit is the launches still in flight after the refused one. A launch that fails for any other reason works as GO-017 says. A test covers: a refusal at 30 running (limit 30, item pending, not counted, not claimed while 30 run, claimed when one finishes); a second refusal at 29 (limit 29); a refusal at limit 1 (counted toward the block).
5. Controlled retest, approved separately. A host limit rises only through a retest, and a retest needs a recorded approval from Dorian: an approving GitHub review by `dorianatmoxywolf` on a pull request whose body names the host, the current limit and the retest ceiling, verified as GO-001.3 verifies a goal approval (reviewer, state `APPROVED`, at the pull request's head). The pull request body names, in these words, host `<host>`, `current limit <L> workers`, `retest ceiling <C> workers` and `dispatch budget $<D> USD`; an approval whose named current limit is no longer the host's limit is refused. `swarm.py init ... --retest --approval <owner>/<repo>#<pr>` lets that run go above the limit, up to the smaller of twice the limit and the number of ready items. `init` refuses `--retest` without an approval that verifies, and records the pull request and review id in the host record. Free text is not an approval. The approval binds exactly what it names: the host, the ceiling and the dispatch budget. `init` refuses a retest on another host, above the approved ceiling, or with a budget above the approved one. Each approval is used once: the host record marks it consumed when `init` accepts it, and `init` refuses any later retest that names it. If the run ends with no host refusal, the limit becomes the larger of the current limit and the most workers confirmed running at once, so a retest that never reaches the current ceiling leaves it unchanged; a capacity refusal still follows criterion 4. Nothing else raises a limit. A test covers a retest with no refusal (limit raised, the review recorded), one with a refusal (limit lowered), `--retest` without an approval, with a review by someone else, and with a review at an older head (each refused); an approval used on another host, above its ceiling, or with a larger budget (each refused); an approval used twice (the second refused); and a run without `--retest` (limit unchanged).
6. A blocked lane stops its dependents only (GO-017, kept). A test covers 30 items in three independent chains with one item blocked: its chain stops and the other two finish.
7. Kept from GO-017: one coordinator merges; workers never merge, review, dispatch, write run state, deploy or see the holdout; `/gstack-peer-review` stays outside the swarm.
8. `DEPS.json` rules that scaling never reopens:
   - Concurrency is not part of an approved plan's meaning. A goal approved before this item merged keeps its `DEPS.json` (including `max_workers` 1 to 3) or its lack of one, unchanged: it runs as approved, with no edit and no re-approval.
   - A goal drafted after this item merged has a `DEPS.json`: `goal_brief.py check` refuses a new goal folder without one, unless it is a hard-cap goal, or a successor whose verified predecessor was approved before this item merged and has no `DEPS.json`; that successor stays serial, and a goal approved after this item merged can't use the exemption. Each `start_after` entry carries a non-empty `because` string (the file, contract or result the later item needs); `check` refuses one without it. The one exception: a successor's legacy string entries are allowed only when its whole `DEPS.json` is verified byte for byte against the `DEPS.json` of an approved predecessor that merged before this item; any new or changed entry needs a non-empty `because`. A fully serial plan is accepted when every entry has its reason.
   - A successor (`--s1`, `--s2`) may carry a `DEPS.json` (superseding GO-017 criterion 7's refusal): it copies its immediate predecessor's (`--s2` from `--s1`) byte for byte, except the entries for items its revised PLAN.md adds or removes; a predecessor with no `DEPS.json` gives a successor with none. `goal_successor.py` refuses any other change to it, including `max_workers`.
   - A hard-cap goal never runs through the swarm: `swarm.py init` and `goal_run.py start` refuse all three markers, and `swarm.py load_plan` keeps refusing the two GO-017 markers. A goal is hard-cap when any of three markers is present: GOAL.md has `## Hard cap`; GO-017's `DEPS.json` key `hard_dollar_cap` is true; or GO-017's rule, GOAL.md's `## Spend cap` says "hard". `swarm.py` keeps refusing the swarm for both GO-017 markers, so an already-approved hard-cap goal can't fall into the swarm. `DEPS.json` is optional for it and, when present, is read for item order and for the `hard_dollar_cap` marker only. `goal_run.py start` and `swarm.py init` refuse all three markers on the swarm path, with that reason, and no paid work starts. A runner that enforces the remaining spend before each billable call is separate work, not part of this item.
   A test covers each rule: an approved goal with `max_workers` 3 accepted unchanged, and one with no `DEPS.json` run as a serial chain; a new goal with no `DEPS.json` (refused); a `start_after` with no `because` (refused); a successor's byte-for-byte copy of a pre-GO-023 predecessor's `DEPS.json` with legacy entries (accepted) and a changed copy of it (refused); a successor that changes `max_workers` (refused) or adds an item's entry (accepted); a hard-cap goal with no `DEPS.json` accepted by `check` and refused by `start` with no billable call made; and each of the three hard-cap markers kept out of the swarm.
9. Proof on the intended host (a host and coordinator proof only). The host is the Cowork cloud setup, where the coordinator session launches Agent workers with the installed gstack-execution plugin, as for real goals. The workload is 30 small synthetic independent items in a scratch repository, each writing its own file and its own passing test.
